**Context:** A research pass across the whole app (not just the auth screens `ScreenLayout` was originally built for) found the same shell duplicated near-verbatim in three shapes: a centered, form-like container (the four auth screens, byte-identical to `ScreenLayout`'s existing implementation); a top-aligned container ending in a `FlatList` (`explore/index`, Home, `programs/index`, `programs/complexes/index`, `programs/exercise-picker`); and a `ScrollView`-based container for single-column detail content (`explore/[id]`, `programs/[id]`, `programs/complexes/[id]` — these three were **100% identical**, not just similar). `ScreenLayout` already existed for exactly this purpose but wasn't consistently adopted, leaving most screens hand-rolling the same `SafeAreaView` + `ThemedView`/`ScrollView` pair it was meant to replace. The audit also surfaced two genuine bugs, not just duplication: `profile/index.tsx` used `Spacing.six` for its top padding and gap where every other screen uses `Spacing.four`, and `profile/[id].tsx` combined `BottomTabInset + Spacing.four` at the container level while also rendering `ProgramsList`, whose own `FlatList` already adds a trailing `Spacing.four` — a real, if small, double bottom-padding.

**Decision:** Rather than introducing a second or third layout component for the list-bearing and scrollable shapes, `ScreenLayout` was extended with three narrow, additive props, all defaulting to its original behavior so no existing consumer changed without an explicit new prop:

- `scroll?: boolean` (default `false`) — swaps the internal `ThemedView` for a `ScrollView` (with `theme.background` applied internally, mirroring what the three detail screens previously did by hand), covering the third shape.
- `justify?: 'center' | 'flex-start'` (default `'center'`) — `'center'` preserves the auth screens' original centered behavior; `'flex-start'` covers every top-aligned screen (lists, the wizard, both profile screens).
- `contentStyle?: StyleProp<ViewStyle>` — an escape hatch for the per-screen values this shell has never standardized (`paddingTop`, `paddingBottom`, and `gap` where a screen genuinely differs from the four-unit default), merged after the base styles, the same role `Button`'s own `style` prop already plays. Each screen's `contentStyle` reproduces only its own actual prior values — nothing was normalized or rounded to a "nicer" shared number where screens didn't already agree (e.g. `auth-callback.tsx` kept its pre-existing `gap: Spacing.three` rather than being pulled to `Spacing.four`).

Future additions to `ScreenLayout` should require at least two real consumers before expanding the public API.

A fourth prop, `align?: 'stretch' | 'center'`, was deliberately **not added** — `auth-callback.tsx` was the only screen needing `alignItems: 'center'`, so it takes that through `contentStyle` instead of widening `ScreenLayout`'s permanent API for a single call site. This follows the same principle the whole sprint was scoped around: consolidate existing duplication into the one component that already existed, but don't grow that component's surface area beyond what's actually earned by more than one real consumer — the same bar this project has applied to every other extraction (`Avatar`, `ProgramsList`, `isNotFoundError`, etc.).

One implementation detail caught during self-review, not during planning: the three original `scroll`-shape screens never had `flex: 1` on their `ScrollView`'s `contentContainerStyle` — only the non-scroll shape's container did. `ScreenLayout`'s shared base style needed splitting (`container` without `flex`, plus a separate `fill` style applied only in non-scroll mode) so adopting `scroll` wouldn't silently introduce a layout property those three screens never had.

Two intentional UI fixes rode along with the adoption, both already called out in the research/plan and approved before implementation: `profile/index.tsx`'s padding/gap normalized to `Spacing.four`, and `profile/[id].tsx`'s bottom padding reduced to `BottomTabInset` alone (removing the redundant `+ Spacing.four` now that `ProgramsList` supplies its own trailing space) — both real, if minor, visual changes, not accidental side effects of the refactor.

**Consequences:** Any future screen shell need should default to `ScreenLayout` with the appropriate `scroll`/`justify`/`contentStyle` combination rather than a new hand-rolled `SafeAreaView` pair — this sprint is the reference for which combination fits which shape. If a fourth genuinely distinct shape or a second real consumer for `alignItems: 'center'` appears later, extending the prop set then is reasonable; adding it speculatively now was not. `programs/create.tsx`'s wizard Cancel now goes through `ScreenHeader` (`onBackPress`, no title) rather than an inline link, matching `exercise-picker.tsx`'s existing Cancel — this closes the last screen in the app that didn't use `ScreenHeader` for its back affordance.

## Program editing, metadata-only: reused wizard, no structural changes, RLS gap still open

**Context:** A full architecture investigation (research doc, approved before implementation) found web's own edit feature non-functional in four distinct ways — its save handler never actually calls its update function (silently creates a duplicate program instead), the dormant update function conflates `exercise_id` with `program_exercises.id` (masked today only because a second bug's fallback happens to compensate), it never deletes days removed by shrinking the day count, and its exercise diffing matches by array position rather than row identity — which would silently reassign historical `training_history`/`exercise_logs` entries to the wrong exercise if it were ever actually exercised. None of this was ported, even in spirit. Separately, live-reconfirmed RLS state (unchanged since Sprint 21) still has no `UPDATE`/`DELETE` policy on `program_days`/`program_exercises`, and every cascade from those tables down through `exercise_drafts`/`exercise_logs`/`training_history` is destructive — so any structural edit (add/remove/reorder days or exercises) needs both a deliberate RLS decision and identity-preserving (not positional) diffing, neither of which this sprint attempts.

**Decision:** Scope was deliberately narrowed to metadata only — `title`/`type`/`level` via a new `updateProgramMetadata(id, userId, updates)` in `programs.ts`, a single `.update()` against `programs` (RLS disabled on that table, confirmed live) that never touches `program_days`/`program_exercises`. The `.eq('user_id', userId)` filter on that update is defense-in-depth, not an enforced constraint — `programs` has no RLS, so this mirrors the same trust model `deleteProgram` already relies on for this exact table, made explicit here rather than a new pattern.

Rather than a parallel edit screen, `programs/create.tsx` was extended with an optional `programId` param (passed from a new ownership-gated "Edit program" action on `programs/[id].tsx`, gated identically to Delete). The existing `useProgramWizardStore` is reused as-is — no new store — since `name`/`type`/`level` are identical in shape to what Create's steps 1–3 already manage; `days`/`daysCount` are simply never read or written in edit mode. Leak-prevention between modes rests on one guarantee: the screen's mount effect always calls `resetWizard()` first, unconditionally, before any conditional prefill runs — so whichever mode last populated the store, the next mount (edit or create) always starts from a fully blank slate before `name`/`type`/`level` are set again. Edit mode's prefill (via the existing `getProgramDetail`) only ever calls `setName`/`setType`/`setLevel` — `days`/`daysCount` stay at `reset()`'s defaults (`[]`/`null`) for the entire session, so there's no path for a prior Create draft's days to survive into an edit, or vice versa. Reset triggers are unchanged from Create's existing three (fresh mount, explicit Cancel, post-success) — no new trigger was added for edit mode.

The wizard's flow caps at step 3 in edit mode: step 4 (days count + exercise picker) is unreachable, and step 3's action becomes "Save changes" (calling `updateProgramMetadata`) instead of advancing further. On success, `router.replace('/programs/${id}')`, mirroring Create's own redirect-to-detail pattern.

One implementation-time correction: the first draft called `setPrefillState` synchronously inside the mount effect, which this project's `react-hooks/set-state-in-effect` convention (state only set inside `.then()`/`.catch()`) correctly rejects. Fixed by relying on `useState`'s initializer for the synchronous "start in loading" case (mirroring `programs/[id].tsx`'s own `id ? {state:'loading'} : ...` pattern) and moving the retry button's synchronous reset into its own event handler, where synchronous `setState` is the established norm.

**Consequences:** Structural editing (add/remove/reorder days or exercises) remains explicitly deferred, blocked on the same RLS gap identified in Sprint 21 and reconfirmed this sprint — that decision (new policies vs. an alternative) still needs to be made deliberately, not assumed, before any future sprint attempts it. Any future edit-mode prefill into this wizard should follow the same reset-first-then-prefill sequencing established here, and any future structural diffing must match by row identity (`program_exercises.id`), never array position — the exact web bug this sprint's research documented as the reason not to.

## Structural program editing: identity-based diff, INSERT+DELETE only, no reorder

**Context:** Sprint 31 deferred structural editing (adding/removing days or exercises) entirely, blocked on the RLS gap first identified in Sprint 21. This sprint resolved it, but not by simply adding back everything web's own edit feature implies — web's actual `updateTrainingProgram` (still dead code, never called by `handleSave`) has four confirmed bugs on top of never running at all: it conflates `exercise_id` with `program_exercises.id` (masked only by a second bug's fallback), never deletes days removed by shrinking the day count, and diffs exercises by array position rather than row identity — which would silently reassign historical `training_history`/`exercise_logs` entries to the wrong exercise if it ever actually ran. None of that logic was ported.

Before implementing, an explicit question was raised and verified rather than assumed: does structural editing actually need an `UPDATE` policy on `program_days`/`program_exercises`, or only `INSERT`/`DELETE`? Answer, confirmed by re-reading the app's own UI: **`program_days` never needs UPDATE** — the day-count control only ever grows/shrinks at the tail (no reorder, no arbitrary-position removal), so an existing day's `day_number` never changes. **`program_exercises` only needs UPDATE if reordering an already-selected exercise must be non-destructive** — since delete+reinsert cascades away that exercise's history before any "replacement" row could exist, there is no way to change an existing slot's position without either an UPDATE or losing its history. Given the exercise-picker has no drag-reorder UI today (the only way to visually reorder is deselect-then-reselect), the explicit decision was made to **not support exercise/day reordering this sprint**, which means every write is either a pure insert or a pure delete — no `UPDATE` policy needed on either table at all.

**Decision:** The wizard store's `days: string[][]` became `WizardDay[]` (`{ id: string | null; exercises: { id: string | null; exerciseId: string }[] }[]`) — `id: null` meaning "not yet persisted." Create mode is simply the case where every id stays null; `createProgram` itself is unchanged, with the wizard mapping the richer shape down to plain exercise-id arrays at its one call site. `exercise-picker.tsx`'s Confirm handler reconciles the final selection against the store's _current_ slots for that day by `exerciseId` (never position), so an exercise that stays selected keeps its real row id regardless of how many times it was toggled within the session or where it ends up in the list — this is what makes the diff safe by construction rather than by convention.

`updateProgramStructure` (replacing Sprint 31's `updateProgramMetadata`, which is now removed — superseded, not left alongside) re-fetches `getProgramDetail` fresh at save time and diffs against it: days beyond the new day count are deleted (cascade covers their exercises/drafts/history — no separate cleanup needed); within kept days, any existing exercise whose id no longer appears is deleted; new days and new exercise slots are inserted, with new exercises always appended after existing ones in a day (`order_index` = current max + 1, ...) since kept rows' `order_index` is never rewritten. One disclosed consequence of the no-reorder decision: a newly-added exercise always lands after all kept exercises in that day, even if the picker showed it interleaved among them — true interleaving would require renumbering kept rows, which the no-UPDATE decision rules out.

A destructive-removal confirmation (reusing the `Platform.OS`-branched `Alert`/`window.confirm` pattern from Program Deletion) fires only when the diff actually finds something to remove, via a `confirmRemoval` callback the data layer calls before writing anything — keeping `programs.ts` free of UI concerns while still doing only one fetch. Returning `false` aborts with zero writes performed (not even the `programs` row update); the call site treats this as a return to idle, not a success or an error.

**Web alignment, checked directly rather than assumed:** web's actual `SelectExercisesStep`/`CreateEditProgram` were read directly, not inferred from the earlier bug research alone. Its day-count-only model (no reorder, no arbitrary removal) already matched what was planned. Its `isValidProgram` check (every day needs ≥1 exercise before the final button enables) was a real rule this app hadn't adopted — now applied to both Create and Edit. Its wizard always enters at step 1 even for edit (no skip-ahead) — adopted here too, reversing an earlier plan to jump straight to step 4, since that friction wasn't actually mobile-specific (web has the identical linear flow). Two things found on web were deliberately **not** ported: it has no removal confirmation anywhere (a direct consequence of its edit path never actually deleting anything, not a considered UX decision — porting "no confirmation" would have shipped mobile's real, working delete with no warning), and its final-step button always reads "Create" even in edit mode (inconsistent, unrelabeled — an artifact of the feature being unfinished, not a decision to match).

**A process note worth recording:** the first implementation of `updateProgramStructure` issued `.delete()` calls against `program_days`/`program_exercises` without first re-verifying that a `DELETE` policy actually existed on either table. It didn't — only `SELECT`/`INSERT` were live, meaning the deletes would have silently no-op'd in production (Supabase doesn't error on a zero-row delete) while the UI reported success. This was caught before committing, not after. The fix was the two policies below, applied live, verified narrower than (not equal to, and never broader than) the existing `INSERT` policies — they reuse only the personal-program-ownership branch (`programs.user_id = auth.uid()`), omitting the trainer/`global_programs` branch INSERT has, since global program editing remains unbuilt and out of scope:

```sql
create policy "Allow delete program_days" on program_days
  for delete using (exists (select 1 from programs p where p.id = program_days.program_id and p.user_id = auth.uid()));

create policy "Allow delete program_exercises" on program_exercises
  for delete using (exists (select 1 from program_days d join programs p on p.id = d.program_id where d.id = program_exercises.day_id and p.user_id = auth.uid()));
```

**Consequences:** Any future write path touching `program_days`/`program_exercises` should verify its required RLS policies live before writing code that depends on them, not after — the same "verify, don't assume" discipline this project applies to schema shape should extend explicitly to policy existence for new operations, which this sprint's process gap shows isn't automatic just because the discipline exists elsewhere. Exercise/day reordering remains unsupported; if it's ever wanted, it needs its own decision (an `UPDATE` policy on `program_exercises` at minimum) rather than being assumed available. `updateProgramMetadata` is gone — any future metadata-only need should extend `updateProgramStructure`'s shape rather than reintroducing a narrower parallel function.

## Settings: non-blocking theme preference, first real caller for `updateProfileById`

**Context:** `updateProfileById`/`ProfileUpdate` were built in this project's very first custom-table sprint but had never had a single caller — no screen let a user change their name or theme. Web's own `/settings` (name editing, split into first/last name fields) and its header `Menu` (a separate, instant-apply dark/light toggle persisted to `profiles.dark`) were the two pieces ported; language selection was explicitly excluded — there's no i18n infrastructure anywhere in this app, and a language picker with no translations behind it would be dead UI, not a feature.

**Decision — theme preference storage and fetch timing:** `themePreference: boolean | null` was added to `useAuthStore` (not a separate store — it's current-user data, exactly what that store already exists to own) rather than gating app startup on fetching it. The first proposal was to fetch it inside `initialize()` and block `RootNavigator`'s render on it, reusing the exact mechanism session restore already uses. That was deliberately reconsidered and rejected during architecture review: unlike session restore, this fetch is sequentially dependent on already knowing the user id, making it a genuine _additional_ network round trip on every cold start, not a one-time cost — paid by every user, including the majority who've never set an explicit preference and would see zero visual difference either way. The adopted design instead fetches in the background, non-blocking: a `refreshThemePreference(userId)` helper (owned entirely by `auth-store.ts`, not `AuthProvider`, which stays a deliberately thin one-shot mounting shim) fires fire-and-forget from both places a session can newly become known — the initial `getCurrentSession()` resolution and the ongoing `subscribeToAuthChanges` callback (a fresh sign-in mid-session needs the same fetch, not just cold-start restore). A stale-response guard (`useAuthStore.getState().user?.id === userId`) discards a response that arrives after the user has signed out or a different user has since signed in. `themePreference` resets to `null` on sign-out, so a second user on a shared device never briefly inherits the previous one's theme.

**Failure behavior, by construction:** because nothing awaits this fetch, there is no path by which a failure (offline, timeout, server error) can leave the app stuck — `.catch()` swallows the error silently and `themePreference` simply stays whatever it already was (`null` on first load), the same OS-scheme fallback a user with no preference gets. No retry loop exists; Settings' own `getProfileById` call (needed anyway to seed the name form) doubles as a natural, low-cost resync point if the background fetch failed or hadn't resolved yet.

**Decision — theme toggle is not optimistic UI, by explicit choice, not oversight:** `handleThemeSelect` calls `updateProfileById` first and only calls `setThemePreference` (the only line that changes what actually renders) inside `.then()`, after the write succeeds. During the request, the only visible change is the pills becoming briefly disabled — colors and pill-selection stay exactly as they were until the server confirms. On failure, `themePreference` was never touched, so there is nothing to roll back; an error line appears and the pills re-enable for the user to retry manually. This was verified against the actual code (not just the intended design) and explicitly confirmed as the preferred behavior — optimistic UI (flipping immediately, reverting on failure) was considered and declined for this screen.

**Decision — shared scheme resolution:** a new `resolveEffectiveScheme(osScheme, preference)` pure function in `use-theme.ts` is now the one source of truth for "what theme is actually in effect" — an explicit preference always wins; the OS scheme is only the fallback when no preference has ever been set. Both `useTheme()` and `_layout.tsx`'s `ThemeProvider` selection (React Navigation's own `DarkTheme`/`DefaultTheme`) now route through it; `_layout.tsx` also switched from calling `react-native`'s raw `useColorScheme` to this app's own custom hook (matching `useTheme()`'s existing source), fixing a small pre-existing inconsistency between the two that had never mattered before (no preference could exist yet) but genuinely could have caused disagreement once one did.

**Decision — name-splitting bug fix, not a straight port:** web seeds its name form via `profile.username?.split(' ')` destructured into exactly `[firstName, lastName]`, which silently drops every word beyond the second for a three-plus-word name — a confirmed bug (how JS array destructuring behaves), not a design choice. Mobile uses `firstName = words[0]`, `lastName = words.slice(1).join(' ')` instead, preserving every word while still recombining identically for the common two-word case — the same "don't port a confirmed bug just because web has it" standard already applied in Program Editing's research. The dirty-check gating Save uses React Hook Form's own `isDirty` rather than a hand-rolled comparison (web's own approach) — a small improvement RHF provides for free, not extra complexity for its own sake.

**Decision — `nameSchema` extraction:** the same first/last-name validation rule (min 3 characters, letters only) existed only inline in `register-form.tsx` until this sprint. Settings became a genuine second consumer, crossing this project's established extraction bar — it's now `src/lib/validation.ts`, imported by both forms instead of duplicated.

**Consequences:** Any future need for "the current user's own data available app-wide, cheaply" should default to extending `useAuthStore` the way `themePreference` did, not introducing a new store, unless the data genuinely doesn't belong to the authenticated-user lifecycle. Any future background fetch on the same pattern should default to non-blocking-with-silent-failure unless a concrete, specific reason requires blocking startup — the reasoning that reversed the original blocking proposal here is the reference case. `resolveEffectiveScheme` is now the only place scheme precedence should ever be computed; a third call site should never reimplement it inline.

## Maintenance 01: removed two unrestricted `friends` RLS policies

**Context:** Social Area research (ahead of Sprint 34) found two policies on `public.friends` scoped to role `public` with `qual: true`: `"Allow realtime delete"` (DELETE) and `"Allow realtime select"` (SELECT) — both named as if required for Supabase Realtime. Neither was: Realtime authorizes row broadcast per-subscriber using a table's SELECT policy alone, never its DELETE policy, and every real client subscribes with an authenticated session, already covered by the separate `"Allow selecting friends for logged-in user"` policy (`qual: true`, `authenticated`). The realtime-scoped SELECT policy was therefore a redundant duplicate of an already-open policy, and the realtime-scoped DELETE policy was strictly harmful — since Postgres OR's together all permissive policies for a command, its unconditional `qual: true` silently overrode the two properly ownership-scoped DELETE policies sitting next to it, letting any client delete any friendship row.

**Decision:** Dropped both policies; added nothing. Re-verified live afterward: DELETE now has exactly the two ownership-scoped policies (`Allow deleting accepted friendships`, `Allow deleting own pending friend requests`); SELECT now has exactly `Allow selecting friends for logged-in user`; INSERT/UPDATE unchanged; `pg_publication_tables` still lists `friends` under `supabase_realtime`, confirming Realtime was unaffected.

**Consequences:** No application code changed — this was a live database-only fix, standalone from Sprint 34. The pre-existing, separately-flagged fully-open SELECT policy (any authenticated user can read any row) and the either-party UPDATE policy (sender or recipient can change `status`) remain untouched and open, out of this task's scope.

## Maintenance 02: added the missing DELETE policy for declining incoming friend requests

**Context:** Sprint 35 research (comparing web's `declineFriendRequest` in `lib/friendData.ts` against live RLS) found that `friends` had exactly two DELETE policies — one for a sender deleting their own pending request (Cancel), one for either party deleting an `accepted` friendship (Remove) — and neither covers the _recipient_ deleting an incoming `pending` row (Decline). Since Supabase doesn't error on a zero-row RLS-filtered delete, web's Decline button has been silently no-oping in production: the UI removes the item locally and reports success, but the row remains in the database forever. This is a live production bug on web today, not a hypothetical mobile risk.

**Decision:** Added `"Allow declining incoming pending friend requests"` — `FOR DELETE TO authenticated USING (friend_id = auth.uid() AND status = 'pending')` — mirroring the existing sender-side policy's shape exactly, scoped to the recipient side instead. Deliberately narrow: it only ever matches a row where the caller is the _recipient_ of a _still-pending_ request, so it cannot be used to delete an accepted friendship or a request the caller merely sent (those remain covered by the other two policies). Re-verified live after applying: all three DELETE policies present, correctly scoped `TO authenticated` (an initial application accidentally defaulted to `PUBLIC` by omitting the `TO authenticated` clause — caught and corrected before this report, not left as a residual gap), and `friends` still present in the `supabase_realtime` publication, confirming no effect on Realtime.

**Consequences:** No application code changed. Mobile's forthcoming Sprint 35 `declineFriendRequest` can now rely on this policy actually deleting the row rather than silently failing — a prerequisite this project's data layer should not have to route around defensively; the query should simply trust that a successful DELETE call means the row is actually gone, since RLS now correctly matches this case.

## Sprint 34, Users & Friends foundation: nested under Profile, no Requests, no Realtime yet

**Context:** The Social Area (Users/Friends/Friend Requests) was the last major web feature area with no mobile equivalent. A dedicated research-and-architecture phase (not summarized here in full) compared web's implementation directly rather than assuming it, live-verified the `friends` schema/RLS, and confirmed `friends` is the only table in this project's `supabase_realtime` publication. Three decisions were made deliberately before implementation:

- **`users.ts` was rejected in favor of extending `profile.ts`.** The initial research proposal split by feature (Users/Friends/Requests) rather than by table — inconsistent with this codebase's actual convention, where `exercises.ts` already keeps `getExercises()` (list-all) and `getExerciseById()` (single row) in one file because they hit the same table. Browsing all users is `profiles` with no `.eq('id', ...)` filter, not a different table — so `getAllProfiles()` was added to `profile.ts`; only `friends.ts` is a new file, since `friends` is a genuinely distinct table.
- **Realtime was deferred**, not because "simpler first" is automatically better, but because the Friend Requests screen — the only feature realtime actually drives — is itself deferred to a later sprint. Building it now would mean designing this app's first-ever subscription lifecycle (mount/unmount, `AppState` foreground/background, sign-out teardown) with no working reference to adapt (web's own subscription has no cleanup at either call site — a confirmed bug, not a pattern to port) in the same sprint as an RLS change touching the same table. When Requests is built, it should sit behind a `{ requests, status, refresh() }`-shaped hook so adding realtime later means patching that hook's internals, not changing the consuming screen.
- **Social was nested under the existing Profile tab, not a fifth `NativeTab`.** Consistent with every prior addition (Settings, Complexes, program editing) — none grew the tab bar. Promoting to a full tab later, if Social grows, is cheap and additive; removing a tab later is the harder, more visible change to walk back.

A standalone maintenance task (documented above) removed two unrestricted `friends` RLS policies before this sprint began, independent of its schedule.

**Decision:** `src/lib/supabase/friends.ts` exports `Friendship`/`getFriendshipsForUser` (both directions in one query), `sendFriendRequest`, and a single `deleteFriendship(friendshipId)` used for **both** "Cancel request" and "Remove friend" — the two ownership-scoped DELETE policies already differentiate by the row's actual status/parties server-side, so the client-side call is identical either way; a second, identically-bodied function would only be duplication. `getFriendshipState(friendships, viewerId, otherUserId)` is a pure helper (alongside the data functions, mirroring `formatProgramType`/`formatProgramLevel` living in `programs.ts`) deriving `'none' | 'outgoing' | 'incoming' | 'accepted'` from the two-directional list. `'incoming'` (someone else's pending request to you) has no action yet — Requests/accept is a later sprint — so it renders as a disabled "Pending" label on the Users screen rather than a misleading button.

Two new screens, `profile/users.tsx` and `profile/friends.tsx`, both added to the existing `profile/_layout.tsx` Stack and linked from `profile/index.tsx` alongside the existing Settings link. A new shared `UserCard` (`src/components/user-card.tsx`) — avatar, name, tap-through to `/profile/[id]`, optional right-side action slot mirroring `ProgramCard`'s existing `badge` slot — is used by both screens rather than building two near-identical rows; it is not built on a shared base "Card" component, since none exists for the two existing card-like components (`ProgramCard`, `UserCard`) to extract into, matching this project's standing preference against premature abstraction. Both screens use `FlatList` (via direct use here, not a `ProgramsList`-style wrapper, since the two screens' rows differ by whether an action slot is passed) rather than a plain mapped list, since `ScreenLayout`'s non-scroll container needs a `FlatList` child to remain scrollable for a long list — the same reason `ProgramsList` itself is a `FlatList`.

Actions on the Users screen are non-optimistic (button disables during the request, refetches `friendships` only — not the whole profiles table — on success), matching this project's established Settings-era default rather than web's optimistic add/cancel. "Remove friend" alone gets a destructive-action confirmation (the same `Platform.OS`-branched `Alert`/`window.confirm` pattern as Program Deletion) since it's the only action here that undoes an existing relationship; "Add friend"/"Cancel request" do not, since neither destroys data web itself doesn't also let you redo trivially.

**Consequences:** Friend Requests (incoming list, accept/decline) and Realtime remain fully unbuilt — the next sprint should build Requests behind the hook-boundary described above, with Realtime as a clean, later addition to that same hook rather than a parallel new sprint concern layered on top. `getAllProfiles`/`getFriendshipsForUser` (whole-table fetches) are the natural reuse point for that sprint rather than new queries.

**Functional testing:** verified on the actual custom development client (`com.corefit.mobile`, rebuilt this same session after finding the previously-installed dev client was stale — see the Android dev-client-vs-Expo-Go troubleshooting note below), not Expo Go. Confirmed working: Users list (ordering, exclusion of the viewer's own row, avatar/initials rendering), Add Friend and Cancel Request (both cross-checked directly against the live `friends` table, not just the UI), row-tap navigation to `/profile/[id]`, back navigation, loading states, and error+retry on both screens (forced via disabling network mid-session). Two scenarios were intentionally left untested rather than seeded with throwaway production data: **Remove Friend** (requires an existing `accepted` friendship, which can only be created via Accept — the Friend Requests sprint's own feature) and the **incoming "Pending" disabled-label state** (requires a second real account sending a request to the test account). Both are recorded here as integration scenarios to verify once the Friend Requests sprint makes them reachable through the UI, rather than being faked via direct data manipulation now.

**Android dev-client note:** during this sprint's testing, the emulator had a stale custom dev client installed (last built long before this session, predating the current `expo`/`react-native` versions), which crashed on launch with `ReferenceError: Property 'MessageQueue' doesn't exist` — a native/JS runtime mismatch, not an app bug. Rebuilding via `npx expo run:android` fixed it. That rebuild also surfaced that `app.config.ts` had never had an explicit `android.package`/`ios.bundleIdentifier` set (this project generates `android/`/`ios/` on demand and neither is committed); `com.corefit.mobile` was chosen as the permanent identifier for both platforms rather than accepting Expo's `com.anonymous.*` placeholder, since a package name is effectively permanent once anything is published. Separately, the same emulator also had Expo Go installed independently of anything this project's tooling did — worth remembering that this project must always be tested via its own custom dev client, never Expo Go, since Expo Go can't run this app's native modules and produces unrelated failures (e.g. an OAuth redirect landing on the production web app instead of the native app, traced to `Linking.createURL`'s Expo-Go-specific dynamic URL never matching Supabase's redirect allow-list) that have nothing to do with mobile's own code.

## Sprint 35: Friend Requests — incoming list, accept/decline

**Context:** Sprint 34 deliberately deferred Friend Requests (the incoming-list, accept/decline half of the Social Area) and Realtime, since both hinge on the same not-yet-built screen. This sprint builds that screen. It relies on Maintenance 02's DELETE policy (`"Allow declining incoming pending friend requests"`, `FOR DELETE TO authenticated USING (friend_id = auth.uid() AND status = 'pending')`) — without it, Decline would have reproduced web's own confirmed-broken silent no-op, exactly the bug Maintenance 02 was written to close ahead of this sprint.

**Decision:** `src/lib/supabase/friends.ts` gained three additions: `getIncomingFriendRequests(userId)` (rows where `friend_id = userId` and `status = 'pending'` — outgoing pending requests stay entirely the Users screen's concern via `getFriendshipState`'s existing `'outgoing'` case, so this query never needs to distinguish direction beyond that filter), `acceptFriendRequest(requestId, currentUserId)` (an `UPDATE ... SET status = 'accepted'`, scoped by both `id` and `friend_id`), and `declineFriendRequest(requestId, currentUserId)` (a `DELETE`, scoped the same way). Both accept and decline re-filter by `currentUserId` client-side even though RLS already scopes both operations to the recipient — matching this project's existing defense-in-depth convention rather than trusting the database layer alone.

A new `profile/requests.tsx` screen (added to `profile/_layout.tsx`'s Stack, linked from `profile/index.tsx`) fetches `getIncomingFriendRequests` and `getAllProfiles` together on focus (via `useFocusEffect`, refetching silently on refocus rather than flashing a loading state — Home's established pattern), renders each request through the existing `UserCard` with an Accept/Decline action pair in its slot, and removes an actioned request from local state immediately on success rather than refetching the list. Friends/Users are left to pick up the resulting change naturally the next time their own `useFocusEffect` fires, rather than this screen reaching into their state.

**Decision — race condition fix in `declineFriendRequest`:** code review surfaced that a plain `.delete()` with no row-count check has a real (if narrow) failure window distinct from the RLS-gap Maintenance 02 fixed: if the request's status moves off `'pending'` between this screen's fetch and the Decline tap (accepted from another device, or from web, since both share the same `friends` table), RLS filters out the now-stale row and Supabase still reports no error — silently deleting zero rows while the caller believes it succeeded, the same silent-failure shape as the pre-Maintenance-02 bug, just from a different cause. Fixed by passing `{ count: 'exact' }` to `.delete()` and throwing when `count === 0`, so the screen surfaces "This request is no longer pending" and refetches instead of optimistically removing a row that was never actually deleted.

**Decision — accept/decline duplication left unaddressed:** `acceptFriendRequest`/`declineFriendRequest` share near-identical shape (same two-parameter signature, same `.eq('id', ...).eq('friend_id', ...)` scoping, differing only in `UPDATE` vs `DELETE`), and `requests.tsx`'s `handleAccept`/`handleDecline` are likewise near-identical one-line wrappers around a shared `withSubmitting` helper. Code review flagged this as cosmetic duplication; it was deliberately left as two functions and two handlers rather than factored into one parameterized helper, since the two operations are semantically different outcomes (a row that persists with new state vs. a row that ceases to exist) and collapsing them behind a shared parameter would obscure that distinction for a two-call-site saving, the same anti-premature-abstraction bar this project has applied elsewhere (`UserCard`/`ProgramCard` staying unmerged in Sprint 34, `deleteFriendship` itself staying a single function specifically because its two call sites *are* semantically identical, unlike this case).

**Consequences:** the `{ requests, status, refresh() }`-shaped hook Sprint 34's doc anticipated for this screen was not extracted — `requests.tsx` keeps its fetch/action state inline, since there is exactly one consumer today and no second call site yet to justify pulling it out. If Realtime is added on top of this screen, extracting that hook at that point (rather than growing the inline state further) is the right moment, not before. Friend Requests and the Social Area's core loop (send → accept/decline → appear in Friends) are now fully built end-to-end; Realtime (auto-refreshing the incoming list without a manual focus/refetch) remains the one deferred piece from Sprint 34's original scope.

**Functional testing:** verified on the same custom dev client (`com.corefit.mobile`) established in Sprint 34. Both Accept and Decline were manually tested end-to-end and passed, cross-checked against the live `friends` table (Accept flips `status` to `accepted` and the row then appears via `getFriendshipsForUser` on Friends; Decline removes the row entirely, confirming Maintenance 02's policy is in effect). `npx tsc --noEmit` passed with no errors. `npx eslint .` reported only the pre-existing `react-hooks/set-state-in-effect` error in `use-color-scheme.web.ts`, unrelated to this sprint's changes and already known from prior sprints.

## Sprint 36: Friend Requests Realtime — live incoming list, no badge yet

**Context:** Sprint 35's doc named Realtime as the one deferred piece of the Social Area's core loop. Web's own equivalent (`store/useFriendRequestStore.ts`) was read directly rather than ported on faith: its `subscribeToRealtime(userId)` is called from two independent, uncleaned-up `useEffect`s (`app/components/header/header.tsx` and `app/requests/components/requests.tsx`), leaking a channel on every mount; and it subscribes with `filter: friend_id=eq.${userId}`, which cannot work for the DELETE case (decline/cancel) — Supabase's Realtime docs confirm `postgres_changes` filters are never evaluated server-side for DELETE events, and separately, because `friends` has RLS enabled, a DELETE's `old` record is stripped to primary-key-only columns regardless of `REPLICA IDENTITY` (live-confirmed: `friends` is `REPLICA IDENTITY DEFAULT`, in the `supabase_realtime` publication, RLS-enabled with an open `SELECT` policy — see Maintenance 01). Both are documented, not guessed: findings came from Supabase's own GitHub discussions/docs, cross-checked against this project's live schema via `supabase db query --linked`.

**Decision:** `src/stores/friend-requests-store.ts` (new) owns `requests`/`phase`/`error`, a `refresh(userId)` fetch via the existing `getIncomingFriendRequests`, and `subscribeToRealtime(userId)` — one unfiltered `postgres_changes` channel (`event: '*'`, no `filter`) on `friends`, with the reasoning above recorded as a code comment at the point the decision is made, not just here. Client-side filtering does the scoping instead: INSERT/UPDATE payloads carry a full `new` row (unaffected by the RLS/replica-identity restriction, which only strips `old`) validated against the already-exported `friendshipSchema`; DELETE is matched by `old.id` — the one column guaranteed present — against the locally-tracked request set. `friendshipSchema` was exported from `friends.ts` for this (previously module-private) so Realtime payloads get the same runtime validation every REST response already gets.

The subscription's lifecycle lives in `AuthProvider` (`src/components/auth-provider.tsx`), not the Requests screen — a second `useEffect`, separate from the pre-existing session-restore one, keyed on `useAuthStore`'s `user?.id` rather than mount. This is the structural fix for web's leak: there is exactly one call site for `subscribeToRealtime` in the whole codebase (verified via grep), so React's guaranteed cleanup-before-next-effect ordering means at most one channel can ever be open, across sign-in, sign-out, and a different user signing in without an app restart — not something bolted on with a dedupe guard. `profile/requests.tsx` was migrated to consume the store (`requests`/`phase`/`error`/`refresh`/`removeRequest`) instead of its own `useState`/`useFocusEffect` fetch; the screen's own `useFocusEffect` now only resyncs the local profile-lookup map (a presentational concern independent of the store), since the requests list itself is kept live by Realtime for as long as the app is signed in, not refetched on every navigation back to the screen.

**Decision — no badge/UI entry point this sprint:** Realtime infrastructure and the UI surface that would consume it (a badge, an entry point outside the Requests screen itself) were deliberately kept in separate sprints, per explicit direction, so a Zustand-store-plus-subscription change and a shared-chrome UI change don't land in the same diff. `NativeTabs.Trigger.Badge`/`badgeValue` were confirmed to exist in `expo-router/unstable-native-tabs` during Sprint 36 planning as the likely mechanism, but nothing was implemented against them here.

**Investigation — Android startup hang, not reproduced:** mid-sprint, Android was reported stuck indefinitely on the initial loading screen (web unaffected). Temporary logging was added at every async step of startup (`authStorage.getItem`, `getCurrentSession`, `auth-store.initialize`, both `AuthProvider` effects) rather than guessing at a fix. Two reproduction attempts against the live emulator — a returning-session cold start and a fully `pm clear`-ed fresh-install cold start — both resolved correctly within ~1s, and `/data/anr/` held no traces mentioning `com.corefit.mobile` (the two present were an unrelated `systemui` GPU/stuck-fence hang and a `com.google.android.youtube` broadcast-receiver timeout, both from the day before). The hang was confirmed no longer reproducible and the investigation was closed without a code change — all temporary logging was removed. Recorded here so a future recurrence isn't re-investigated from zero: it was never root-caused, only found to be transient/environmental at the time.

**Consequences:** the Social Area's core loop (send → accept/decline → appear in Friends) is now fully live end-to-end without a manual refresh, closing the item Sprint 34 and 35 both deferred. This is the first Realtime usage anywhere in this codebase — any future subscription should default to the same shape (single call site in `AuthProvider` keyed on session identity, unfiltered channel with client-side scoping if RLS is enabled on the table) rather than a per-screen `useEffect`. A badge/entry-point UI parity pass between web and mobile (header badge, navigation, entry points) is the explicit next piece of work, not yet started.

**Functional testing:** verified across both platforms — Web (`expo start --web`) and the Android custom dev client (`com.corefit.mobile`) — with a live cross-platform request sent in both directions (Web → Mobile and Mobile → Web), followed by Accept and by Decline, confirming the recipient's Requests screen updates live with no manual refresh in every case. A sign-out/sign-in cycle was exercised to confirm the channel doesn't leak. Separately confirmed via `supabase.getChannels().length` (a temporary check, removed afterward) that exactly one channel exists after sign-in — no duplicate subscription from `AuthProvider`'s `userId`-keyed effect firing once with `undefined` and once with the real id before the guard. `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint .` both clean (only the known pre-existing `use-color-scheme.web.ts` error).

## Sprint 37: Friend Requests badge — cross-platform, interim entry point

**Context:** a full Web ↔ Mobile UI parity review (not summarized in full here) compared web's header/navigation against mobile's, ahead of adding the badge Sprint 36 deferred. Working from an explicit "products evolve together" directive — not pixel parity, but the same behavior, information architecture, and navigation meaning, with platform-native presentation — three questions were resolved before writing any code: whether web should also move from presence-only to a numeric count, whether the count belongs in new store state, and whether either platform's entry point should be restructured into a proper labeled navigation item.

**Decision — count, not presence, on both platforms:** web's existing header icon only ever indicated presence (`requests.length > 0`, no number). Under the parity directive, a numeric badge on mobile next to a presence-only icon on web would itself be a new inconsistency — same underlying fact, different fidelity. Numeric badges are also the native convention on both iOS and Android (`NativeTabs.Trigger.Badge`), so matching that on web isn't porting mobile's mechanism, it's aligning what the number *means*. The only counter-argument found (a numeric pill is a small design lift over a static icon swap) wasn't judged strong enough to keep the information architecture inconsistent.

**Decision — no new state:** both platforms read `requests.length` directly at the point of use (mobile: a `useFriendRequestsStore` selector in `app-tabs.tsx`; web: `useFriendRequestStore()`'s existing `requests` in `header.tsx`) rather than deriving/storing a separate count anywhere. `friend-requests-store.ts` gained nothing — it still owns only `requests`/`phase`/`error`/`refresh`/`removeRequest`, unchanged since Sprint 36.

**Decision — clamp at 99+:** both platforms render `count > 99 ? '99+' : String(count)` (mobile) / `count > 99 ? '99+' : count` (web) independently — a one-line rule duplicated rather than shared, since the two platforms are different languages in different repos and the rule is too small to be worth a cross-repo abstraction.

**Decision — a labeled/redesigned navigation entry was explicitly considered and deferred, not built:** the parity review's own recommendation was to give Requests a proper labeled nav item (in `HeaderNavigation`/`Menu`) so it reads unambiguously as navigation rather than an icon riding on header chrome. That was pulled back before implementation, twice, by explicit direction: this sprint's job is the badge, not a header/navigation redesign, and bundling the two would repeat the exact infra-vs-UI scope-mixing Sprint 36 was careful to avoid. **Both platforms' current entry points — mobile's badge on the Profile tab, web's badge on the existing header icon — are therefore interim.** Neither Requests nor the broader Social area (Friends, Users, Requests together) has a dedicated navigation destination on either platform yet; the badge rides on whatever's nearest (Profile tab on mobile, the existing icon on web) until that changes. A future sprint redesigning navigation for Social should treat this as the trigger to revisit both badges' placement, not just add to them.

**Implementation:** `src/components/app-tabs.tsx` — `NativeTabs.Trigger.Badge` on the Profile trigger, shown only when `pendingRequests > 0`. Web's `app/components/header/header.tsx` — the existing Requests icon `<Link>` is now unconditionally rendered (previously gated behind `requests.length > 0`, meaning the icon itself used to disappear at zero with no nav path back to `/requests` at all); a new `.addBadge` pill (`header.module.scss`) overlays it, shown only when count > 0. `HeaderNavigation.tsx` and `Menu.tsx` are untouched. Note: `.add` (the icon wrapper's className) had no corresponding rule in `header.module.scss` before this sprint — it resolved to no class at all — so `position: relative` had to be added there for the badge's `position: absolute` to anchor against, a necessary side effect of making the overlay work, not a pre-existing style being changed.

**Consequences:** the CoreFit web repo (`github.com/YuriiDzoma/CoreFit`, separate repo, no `docs/` of its own — this entry is the record for both sides of this change) now has a numeric badge instead of a presence dot, and its Requests icon is always visible for the first time — closing the "no nav path at zero" gap the parity review found. A labeled navigation entry for Requests/Social remains the next real UX improvement on both platforms, deliberately not this sprint's.

**Functional testing:** `npx tsc --noEmit` clean and `npx eslint .` clean (known pre-existing `use-color-scheme.web.ts` error only) on `CoreFit-mobile`. `npx tsc --noEmit` clean on `CoreFit`; `next lint` isn't actually configured there (it prompts an interactive ESLint setup wizard) — no working lint check existed for this repo before or after this change.

## Sprint 38: Friends parity — preview + per-user Friends list

**Context:** a dedicated architecture review (not summarized here in full) compared web's `Friends.tsx`/`Friends.server.tsx`/`AllFriends.tsx` against mobile's own-only `profile/friends.tsx` and confirmed the data layer needed zero changes — `getFriendshipsForUser` already accepted any user id, and `friends`' `SELECT` RLS was already open to any authenticated user (Maintenance 01). The gap was entirely UI: no way to see another user's friends, and no preview anywhere on either profile screen.

**Decision — single screen, optional param, no new route:** `profile/friends.tsx` gained an optional `userId` query param (`useLocalSearchParams`) rather than a nested `profile/[id]/friends.tsx` route, which would have required converting the existing `profile/[id].tsx` file into a folder for no real benefit — expo-router apps don't expose these paths to end users the way web URLs do. `isOwnProfile = !paramUserId || paramUserId === user?.id` mirrors `profile/[id].tsx`'s own established ownership-branch pattern exactly, including a self-referencing `?userId=<own id>` degrading safely to the own-profile case rather than needing special handling.

**Decision — `resolveFriendProfiles` extracted to `friends.ts`:** the friendship-row-to-profile resolution logic (filter to `accepted`, resolve the other party's id, look up via a `getAllProfiles()` map, drop unresolvable rows) now has three call sites — the Friends screen and both profile screens' preview — crossing this project's established extraction bar. A friendship whose other party has no matching profile is silently dropped, the same defensive behavior `profile/friends.tsx` already had before this sprint.

**Decision — hide the preview entirely at zero friends, both own and another's profile, no CTA:** considered and explicitly rejected an empty-state nudge ("browse users to add friends") on the own-profile case — `Users` already serves as the discovery entry point, and a second CTA would duplicate navigation and clutter the Profile screen. Revisit only if user testing later shows a real discoverability gap.

**Decision — `FriendsPreview` (`src/components/friends-preview.tsx`, new):** went through two design iterations after manual testing found the first version didn't read as distinct from the full list. Final design: a compact grid item (circular avatar, username below wrapping to up to 2 lines, no chevron) — deliberately not built on `UserCard`, matching web's own preview treatment (avatar-above-name grid) being visually distinct from its full list (avatar-beside-name rows), not a pixel port of it. Fixed at exactly 3 items regardless of total count, by explicit decision — this stays a preview, not a second full list, unlike web's variable ~2-row wrap (which can show more). The whole block is one `Pressable` (→ the full list) with each item's own nested `Pressable` (→ that friend's profile) — React Native's touch responder system resolves the two correctly with no manual handling; this was an explicit ask to diverge from web, where only the "See all friends" text itself is a link, not the whole block. `totalCount` is a separate prop from `friends.length` on purpose, so a future upstream truncation can't silently reproduce a confirmed web bug: `Friends.server.tsx` caps its profile fetch at `.limit(12)` but still displays that capped length as the total, undercounting any user with 13+ friends.

**Decision — dynamic "{name}'s Friends" title kept, diverging from web:** web's full list page always titles itself just "Friends," relying on its persistent header/URL to carry context. Mobile has no equivalent persistent context once inside the full-screen Friends list, so a bare "Friends" title after tapping into someone else's list risked feeling disorienting — kept as a deliberate, considered mobile improvement, not a reflexive copy of web's string.

**Bug found and fixed — expo-router initial-route remount race:** manual testing surfaced a reproducible "Can't perform a React state update on a component that hasn't mounted yet" warning on cold start. Root-caused (not guessed at) via `git stash` bisection across individual files plus repeated baseline runs to rule out flakiness (pre-Sprint-38 code: 0/3 runs; with `friends.tsx`'s changes: reproducible every run). Mechanism: when `profile/friends.tsx` is the route expo-router restores directly on cold start, it briefly mounts a transient instance that unmounts and remounts before the route settles; the transient instance's in-flight fetch can resolve after its own unmount, racing the real instance's mount. Fixed with a per-instance `canSetStateRef` (`useRef(false)`, set `true`/`false` in a mount effect and its cleanup) guarding both `setLoadState` call sites — verified fixed across 3 repeated cold-start runs, 0 warnings each.

**Parity review outcome (two rounds, post-implementation):** first-pass manual testing found the feature worked but didn't read as close enough to web. A line-by-line re-comparison against the actual web CSS (not assumption) surfaced one concrete correction — web's Friends-related avatars are plain squares (`.friends__link img`/`allFriends.module.scss` have no `border-radius` at all), inconsistent with the 4px-rounded squares web uses everywhere else (Users list, Home feed, main profile) — almost certainly a web oversight, not a deliberate choice, and not worth porting given mobile's own consistent circular-avatar convention used everywhere. Everything else reviewed was classified as either already matching, an already-approved intentional mobile improvement, or an acceptable platform difference (native back button vs. none; mobile's own page being structurally busier around the widget than web's, since mobile has no persistent header/nav bar and has to put Friends/Requests/Users/Settings links somewhere on-screen). The one concrete adjustment from that review — usernames wrapping to 2 lines instead of truncating to 1 (matching web's `-webkit-line-clamp: 2`) — was applied, along with `alignItems: 'flex-start'` on the grid row so one two-line name doesn't stretch its shorter neighbors.

**Consequences:** manual testing after the second parity round found the remaining "doesn't quite feel like web" gap wasn't `FriendsPreview` anymore — it's the app shell itself (header, navigation, spacing, overall page structure) having drifted from web's, a much larger surface than this sprint's scope. Explicitly deferred to its own dedicated App Shell parity sprint rather than continuing to iterate on `FriendsPreview`, which is now considered done.

**Functional testing:** `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint .` clean (known pre-existing `use-color-scheme.web.ts` error only) after every round of changes, verified repeatedly. The React state-update warning fix was verified via repeated device cold-start tests (3 runs clean), not a single pass. Full manual click-through (own profile preview, another user's profile preview, tap-through to a friend's profile vs. tap-through to the full list, own vs. another's Friends screen title/back/empty-state copy) was performed by the user directly on the Android emulator across three iterations (initial implementation, grid redesign, 2-line wrap) — I don't have a UI-driving tool for this project, so all visual/interactive confirmation for this sprint came from the user, not from me.

## Sprint 39: The Continuous Workspace — introduced and validated on Home

**Context:** a dedicated App Shell parity arc (research-only, not summarized in full here) compared web's and mobile's page composition — not component-by-component, but as complete visual compositions — and converged on a specific design direction: a "Continuous Workspace," a single surface spanning from the top safe-area inset through to the tab bar, replacing `ScreenLayout`'s per-screen, disconnected background painting. Three design concepts were evaluated (a bordered header+panel, a rounded "sheet," and a lightweight rhythm-only approach); the sheet-derived "Workspace" direction was chosen and then explicitly reframed, at the user's request, from "a sheet placed on the screen" to "the screen's own continuous surface" — a real responsibility consolidation, not a naming change: one component absorbing safe-area handling, the surface, and content composition, rather than a background layer collaborating with `ScreenLayout` as two separate systems.

**Decision — `Workspace` (`src/components/workspace.tsx`, new) replaces `ScreenLayout` as the long-term shell, migrated one screen at a time:** structurally near-identical to `ScreenLayout` (same `SafeAreaView` shell, same `scroll`/`justify`/`contentStyle` prop API) so migration is close to a mechanical swap — the one substantive change is a new `workspace` theme token (`theme.ts`) instead of `background`. `ScreenLayout` was deliberately left completely untouched rather than adapted into a compatibility layer — with only one screen migrating this sprint, two components coexisting side by side (one new, one unchanged) was simpler and lower-risk than building a shim neither screen actually needed yet. Implemented in seven small, independently-verified steps per explicit instruction (token → component in isolation → Home migration → device verification → tab bar sync → device verification → confirm no leftover compatibility code), each checked with `tsc`/`eslint` and, where relevant, real Android screenshots before moving to the next.

**Bug found and fixed along the way — `app-tabs.tsx`'s tab bar was never actually theme-aware:** syncing the tab bar's tone to the new `workspace` token appeared to do nothing on the device. Root cause, confirmed by testing an unmistakable color (`#FF0000`) before trusting any subtler value: `app-tabs.tsx` read React Native's raw `useColorScheme()` directly, never this app's own `useTheme()` (which layers a signed-in user's explicit `profiles.dark` preference on top of the OS scheme, via `resolveEffectiveScheme`). Every other screen already read theme through `useTheme()`; the tab bar alone read the OS scheme directly, so on a device where the OS scheme and the in-app preference disagreed, the tab bar silently rendered the wrong theme entirely — pre-existing since this component was first written, invisible until a tab-bar color needed to actually agree with a screen's resolved theme. Fixed by switching `app-tabs.tsx` to `useTheme()`, the same pattern every other screen already uses.

**Design investigation — the panel-border hypothesis, tested and retired for Home:** post-implementation review found Home visually closer to web but still not matching the "contained" feeling web's own Home has. The initial hypothesis — that web's containment comes primarily from `.content`'s bordered panel (`app.module.scss`) — was tested empirically rather than assumed, in two rounds, both via real temporary A/B edits to the live web app (never committed; confirmed reverted via `git diff` after each round):

1. On the Sign In screen (the only screen reachable without credentials), progressively stripping the panel border, then every border on the page (inputs, button). Structure survived even at zero borders, carried by the global header and the page's own centered heading — but this screen has two strong anchors Home doesn't have, so the result couldn't be generalized to Home directly.
2. A second, more targeted test isolated exactly the variable in question on Home itself. Since reaching the authenticated Home feed requires credentials this project doesn't have, two temporary, fully self-contained routes were built (`design-test-home-bordered`/`-noborder`) replicating Home's real composition — real `Navigation` and `trainingHistories.module.scss` card styling, mock data — with the *only* difference between them being whether `.content`'s border rendered. No shared file (`AppShell`, `Header`, `Navigation`, or any shared CSS) was modified to build this — `AppShell` wraps every route including test ones in its own real `.container`/`.content`, so the border-only variant used a scoped inline `<style>` tag targeting the imported `.content` class name directly, live only on that one temporary route.

Removing the panel border on Home changed almost nothing visible: each `historyCard` already carries its own 1px border (`trainingHistories.module.scss`, untouched by the test), so content was already individually structured independent of any outer panel border. This **retires the panel-border hypothesis for Home specifically** — mobile's own feed cards are already individually bordered too (`src/app/(app)/index.tsx`, unchanged this sprint), so the outer-border question is close to a non-factor on the one page-type both platforms actually share this comparison on.

**Consequences — the conclusion reframes Sprint 40 around composition, not a missing element:** web's contained feeling isn't produced by any single dominant device (border, header, or otherwise) — it's the cumulative effect of several small structural cues that each individually contribute little but compound: header rhythm, navigation rhythm, the transition into content, the workspace surface itself, and (not yet built) lighter cards. Mobile's Home today has only one of those layers doing real work (its own already-bordered cards); the others are either absent (no header/nav rhythm on native, by deliberate design) or not yet layered in (`Workspace` exists but cards haven't been lightened to work with it). Sprint 40 should therefore treat "make it feel more contained" as a composition problem to accumulate small cues toward, not a search for one missing visual element — concretely: the card-lightening pass (deferred from Sprint 39 specifically so the workspace surface could be evaluated in isolation first), `Workspace` rollout to additional screens starting with Profile (the next real test of the concept outside a pure card-list screen), and continued attention to spacing/rhythm rather than another pass at border or tone alone.

**Functional testing:** `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint .` clean (known pre-existing `use-color-scheme.web.ts` error only) after every step. Each of the seven implementation steps was verified independently — device screenshots for the visual steps, confirmed no FATAL crashes via `adb logcat`, `git diff`/`git status` confirming no unintended files touched at each checkpoint. The `app-tabs.tsx` theme bug was verified fixed via a real device screenshot after the fix, not assumed from the code change alone. Both rounds of the web border investigation were performed against the actual live web app (a local dev server, headless Chrome screenshots at phone width), not simulated or reasoned about from CSS alone; all temporary web-repo changes across both rounds were confirmed fully reverted via `git diff` before this entry was written.

## Phase 1, PR 1: Workspace — additive top-edge safe-area capability

**Context:** the first PR of Phase 1, "App Shell & Chrome Ownership," from the frozen Web → Mobile Parity Master Plan, Technical Architecture Master Plan, and Phase 1 Implementation Specification (produced this sprint, not summarized here). Phase 1's target architecture introduces a new composition primitive rendering the App Bar (`Header`) and `Workspace` as siblings, rather than `Header` living as a child inside `Workspace`'s own content the way it does today on all four primary tabs. That requires `Workspace`'s existing `SafeAreaView` to stop unconditionally claiming the top edge, since a future sibling will own it instead. This PR ships only that one capability, in isolation, with nothing yet depending on it — per the Implementation Specification's explicit PR 1 scope.

**Decision — additive opt-in, not a new default:** `Workspace` gained one new prop, `topInset` (boolean, default `true`). When `true` or omitted — every existing caller, all 20 of them across the four primary tabs, every pushed/secondary screen, and every auth screen — `SafeAreaView` receives no `edges` prop at all, identical to its behavior before this PR. Only `topInset={false}` changes anything, passing `edges={['right', 'bottom', 'left']}` instead, and nothing in the codebase passes it yet. This preserves `Workspace`'s existing prop-API backward compatibility exactly, as the specification required.

**Implementation:** `src/components/workspace.tsx` only — a new `topInset` prop on `WorkspaceProps`, and a computed `edges` value passed to both the scroll and non-scroll `SafeAreaView` instances. No screen file, no other component, changed.

**Consequences:** unlocks PR 2 (introducing the new `Screen` composition primitive), which will be the first real consumer of `topInset={false}`. No visible or behavioral change to any of the 20 current `Workspace` consumers — confirmed, not assumed, by isolating the diff to this one file before writing this entry.

**Functional testing:** `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint` clean on the modified file. `git diff` confirmed the change is isolated to `workspace.tsx` alone, with no other file touched.

## Home screen visual parity — card composition, emphasis, and skeleton loading

**Context:** a live, on-device audit (Android emulator, real signed-in data) comparing Home against web's actual `trainingHistories.tsx`/`.module.scss` surfaced that architecture work alone hadn't closed the visual gap — Home's activity cards still didn't read as CoreFit. This work is deliberately scoped to layout, spacing, hierarchy, typography, and composition only; no business logic, Supabase queries, routing, stores, or APIs were touched.

**Bug found and fixed — Home's cards had the same nested-opaque-fill problem `ProgramCard` had before Sprint 40, but it was never applied here:** `card`, `cardHeader`, and `exerciseList` in `src/app/(app)/index.tsx` were plain `ThemedView`s with no `backgroundColor` override, each defaulting to an opaque `theme.background` fill nested inside `Workspace`'s `theme.workspace` surface — up to three mismatched, stacked rectangles per card, on the first screen a user sees. Fixed with the same `backgroundColor: 'transparent'` treatment already approved for `ProgramCard`, applied to `card`, `cardHeader`, `exerciseList`, and `errorBlock` for consistency. Confirmed live in both themes on the emulator — most visible in dark mode, where `background` (`#0F172A`) and `workspace` (`#212225`) diverge the most.

**Decision — card header recomposed to match web's actual structure:** web's `historyCard__header` is a centered, stacked column (avatar+username row, then a "Finished · date" row, 16px apart) — not a space-between row. Mobile's `cardHeader` changed from `flexDirection: 'row', justifyContent: 'space-between'` to a centered column (`alignItems: 'center', gap: Spacing.three`), with a new `dateRow` sub-block pairing a "Finished" label with the date, matching web's `.datBox` exactly (copy-only addition, no new data).

**Decision — text emphasis rebalanced to match web:** web bolds the exercise value (`.exerciseList__weight`, weight 700) and leaves the username plain (`p`, weight 400) — mobile had this backwards, bolding the username (`smallBold`) and leaving values flat. Swapped: username is now `type="small"`, and each exercise line splits into a plain name plus a nested `smallBold` value, matching web's `{name}: <span class="weight">{value}</span>` structure via React Native's native support for nested `Text`.

**Decision — card padding tightened to match web's density:** web's `.historyCard` padding is 8px; mobile's was 16px, twice as loose. Changed `card.padding` from `Spacing.three` to `Spacing.two`, an existing token already in the scale.

**Decision — loading state replaced with a shape-matched skeleton:** web shows `NewsSkeleton` (shimmering card-shaped placeholders) while mobile showed a bare "Loading activity…" text line. Added a local `HomeCardSkeleton` (unexported, used only in this file) reusing the real card's exact styles with gray `backgroundElement`-colored blocks in place of content — three stacked instances render during the loading state instead of the text line. No shimmer animation; static blocks only, matching this pass's "polish, not redesign" scope.

**Consequences:** a further, broader Home-level review (composition, vertical rhythm, safe area, header positioning, first viewport, scrolling, hierarchy) was started against this updated baseline. One additional finding surfaced and is pending a decision before implementation: the outer card's own gap between its header block and its exercise-list block is `Spacing.two` (8px) versus web's equivalent `historyCard { row-gap: 16px }` — not part of this round's approved scope, flagged for the next pass rather than fixed opportunistically here.

**Functional testing:** `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint` clean. Verified live on the Android emulator in both light and dark theme (real authenticated data, not mocked) — confirmed the transparent-fill fix, the recomposed header, the rebalanced emphasis, the tighter padding, and the skeleton's shape all render as intended before this entry was written.

## Stage 1, item 1: Header parity — measured against web, not estimated

**Context:** the project entered a new phase, "Stage 1: Web → Mobile parity" — the explicit target is visual, behavioral, and structural parity with the web application, pixel-perfect wherever React Native allows it. The web app is treated as the design specification; platform-native substitutions are only acceptable where a real technical limitation exists (e.g. no hover on touch), never for "better mobile UX" alone. Priority order for this phase: Header → Menu → Workspace/content container → screen-by-screen visual parity → Navigation (deliberately last, given its architectural size).

**Measurements taken directly from web source** (`header.module.scss`, `header.tsx`, and the actual SVG assets — not estimated): at web's own mobile-width breakpoint (≤768px), the wordmark is 20px (not 28px, which is desktop-only); the header row itself carries no vertical padding at all — its 16px of visual separation from what follows is a `margin-bottom` on the row, not internal padding; the menu icon renders at 32×32px. Its color was checked against the actual SVG source rather than assumed from the light/dark filename convention: `menuMob.svg` (used in dark theme) is solid `#ffffff`, `menuMobDark.svg` (light theme) is solid `#19355A` — both exact matches for `theme.text`, not `theme.textSecondary`.

**Implementation (`src/components/header.tsx`):** wordmark font size corrected from `type="default"`'s 16px to 20px via a `wordmark` style override. Menu icon size corrected from 22 to 32; tint corrected from `theme.textSecondary` to `theme.text`. Removed the row's `paddingVertical: Spacing.three` entirely — web's row has none, and the space after it was already separately covered by each screen's own `contentStyle.gap`, so removing it doesn't lose any spacing, it removes a doubled one.

**Consequences:** two related findings surfaced but are explicitly out of scope for this item, deferred to their own place in the priority order — `Workspace`'s horizontal padding (`Spacing.four`, 24px) measures three times wider than web's actual mobile-width container padding (8px), which affects Header's rendered position but is a `Workspace`-level property, not Header's own (belongs to item 3, "Workspace/content container parity"). The menu button's behavior (full navigation vs. web's in-place dropdown) and Friend Requests' placement remain unresolved, each scoped to their own later item.

**Functional testing:** `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint` clean. Verified live on the Android emulator (dark theme, real data) — wordmark size, menu icon size/color, and the tightened vertical spacing all confirmed rendering as measured before this entry was written.

## Stage 1, item 2: Menu parity — in-place dropdown, not a navigation

**Context:** web's hamburger menu (`menu.tsx`) expands a dismissible panel in place — the page never changes. Mobile's previously navigated to Settings entirely, a materially different interaction, not just a styling difference.

**Measurements taken directly from web source** (`header.module.scss`'s `.menu__show`/`.menu__content`/`.shadow`, `menu.tsx`, and the actual icon SVGs): panel height 107px, animation duration 150ms (`transition: all .15s`), backdrop `rgba(0,0,0,0.3)`, panel border 2px solid `theme.border`, border-radius 4px, panel background `var(--bg-color)` (`theme.background`, not `workspace` or `backgroundElement`), content padding 20px vertical / 24px horizontal, row-gap 20px between the two menu rows, text 16px. Panel content is exactly two rows: a "Settings" link and a single theme-toggle control (not a two-option picker — that's Settings' own, separate control).

**Implementation (`src/components/header.tsx`):** the menu button now toggles a `Modal`-based overlay instead of navigating. `Modal` is the one native API used here out of a real technical limitation, not preference — React Native has no direct equivalent of CSS `position: fixed` covering the viewport, so some portal-like mechanism is required to escape the Header's own layout bounds the way web's backdrop escapes its DOM position. Panel position is computed via `measureInWindow` on the button itself (not guessed), since there's no implicit "anchor to parent" relationship once content renders through a root-level `Modal`. Both the panel height and the backdrop opacity animate via a single shared `Animated.Value`, 150ms, matching web's timing and easing intent as closely as RN's `Animated` API allows. The theme toggle reuses the exact same `updateProfileById`/`setThemePreference` calls already used on the Settings screen, as a single flip of the current effective scheme — matching web's `handleToggleTheme`, not Settings' own two-pill picker.

**Consequences:** Settings' own theme toggle (the two-pill Light/Dark picker) was left in place, not removed — web's Settings page has no theme control at all (confirmed in the earlier shell audit), so this is technically an extra access point mobile has that web doesn't. Removing it would be a Settings-page-parity change, not a Menu-parity one, and wasn't part of this item's scope; flagged here rather than decided unilaterally.

**Functional testing:** `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx eslint` clean (one `react-hooks/refs` error surfaced from reading an `Animated.Value` during render via `useRef` — fixed by switching to `useState`'s lazy initializer, which carries the same stable-across-renders guarantee without the lint rule's ref-read concern). Verified live on the Android emulator: open animation, correct panel border/background/spacing, Settings row, theme row with the correct dark/light icon, and backdrop-tap-to-dismiss all confirmed working before this entry was written.

## Stage 1, item 3: Workspace/content container parity

**Context:** audited in full-screen context (Safe Area → Header → content container → first viewport), not in isolation, per this phase's explicit requirement.

**Measurements taken directly from web source** (`app.module.scss`, both the base rules and the `@media max-width:769px` block — web's own mobile-width breakpoint): `.container` padding is 8px at mobile width (not the 16px/24px desktop value); `.content` (nested inside `.container`, wrapping only page content — never Header or Navigation) has a 2px `border-color` border, 4px border-radius, and 8px padding of its own, separate from `.container`'s.

**Implementation (`src/components/workspace.tsx`):**
1. `paddingHorizontal` corrected from `Spacing.four` (24px) to `Spacing.two` (8px) — a real, three-times-too-wide discrepancy already flagged during Header parity. Affects all 20 current `Workspace` consumers at once, since this is a shared-component change, not a per-screen one.
2. Added a new inner `content` box reproducing web's `.content` exactly — 2px border (`theme.border`), 4px radius, 8px padding — wrapping `children` inside the existing outer container.

**Known, tracked imperfection — not a new decision:** web's `.content` box wraps only page content; Header and Navigation sit outside it. `Workspace` doesn't yet have that same chrome/content separation — Header still renders as `children`'s first element on the four primary tabs (Phase 1's `Screen` composition primitive, which would fix this properly, was scoped and then explicitly deferred earlier this project).
